Well, it's... it's just been a lot lately. Ever since those foreclosure notices started coming in the mail, I feel like I'm just coming apart at the seams. I don't really know about... like, actual panic episodes? I'm not entirely sure what counts as that, honestly. I just get really wound up, you know? Like my stomach just drops when I see the mail truck pull up. I actually helped him patch a hole in his exhaust a few years back because he was getting fumes in the cab and the fleet mechanic was backed up. Good guy, Gary. But... wait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about the bank?

I don't think so? I mean, I wouldn't say I feel physically restless or like I'm constantly looking over my shoulder for something bad to happen. I don't really have that. The guy running the sale was moving down to Arizona, taking this massive Class A motorhome down there. It was a Winnebago, one of the big diesel pushers. Had a Cummins ISB 6.7-liter engine in it, which is a really solid block, but I had to warn him about the emissions systems on those. The DPF filters get completely choked up with soot if you just idle them or putt around town. You really have to get them up to highway speeds so the exhaust gets hot enough to run a proper regen cycle, or else you're looking at a massive repair bill and you're stuck in limp mode on the side of the highway. I even wrote down the specific fuel additive he needed on a piece of cardboard for him to keep in the glovebox...

But yeah, I'm just jumping out of my skin every time my phone rings. And at work, I'm snapping at the new apprentices, which isn't fair to them. They're just kids, really. One of them, Tommy, reminds me of my nephew. Always wearing his hat backwards and dropping his wrenches. But I'm making stupid mistakes in the repair bay, too—stuff I never would've messed up before. Just completely losing my focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or have episodes of intense worry or panic, especially related to the stress with the foreclosure?\n\nPatient: I don't really know about... like, actual panic episodes? I'm not entirely sure what counts as that, honestly. I just get really wound up, you know? Like my stomach just drops when I see the mail truck pull up. Our carrier, Gary, he's this older guy who always wears those regulation postal shorts with the knee-high socks, even when it's freezing out. He's been doing our route for fifteen years, maybe more. He drives one of those old Grumman LLVs, those boxy little mail trucks? They have the old Iron Duke engine in them, which is practically indestructible if you keep the oil clean, but the chassis on those things rust out so bad around here because of all the road salt in the winter. I actually helped him patch a hole in his exhaust a few years back because he was getting fumes in the cab and the fleet mechanic was backed up. Good guy, Gary. But... wait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about the bank?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel physically restless or on edge, like you can't relax or you're constantly watching for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: I don't think so? I mean, I wouldn't say I feel physically restless or like I'm constantly looking over my shoulder for something bad to happen. I don't really have that. I try to just sit and relax when I finally get home. I have this really great old La-Z-Boy recliner I bought at an estate sale a few years back. \n\nThe guy running the sale was moving down to Arizona, taking this massive Class A motorhome down there. It was a Winnebago, one of the big diesel pushers. Had a Cummins ISB 6.7-liter engine in it, which is a really solid block, but I had to warn him about the emissions systems on those. The DPF filters get completely choked up with soot if you just idle them or putt around town. You really have to get them up to highway speeds so the exhaust gets hot enough to run a proper regen cycle, or else you're looking at a massive repair bill and you're stuck in limp mode on the side of the highway. I even wrote down the specific fuel additive he needed on a piece of cardboard for him to keep in the glovebox... \n\nBut anyway, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about relaxing?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason or specific problem to focus on?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / heavy_vehicle_or_mobile_equipment_service_technician_or_mechanic / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really... I don't just worry about random stuff for no reason. It's really just the bank and the house that's eating me alive right now. Normally, I'm pretty good at just focusing on what's in front of me and not stressing over things that aren't there. You kind of have to be in my line of work. 

Like, just this past Tuesday, we had this massive Caterpillar D8 dozer hauled into the yard. The undercarriage was completely shot. I mean, the track tension was all out of whack, and the sprockets were just chewed to pieces. The guy who was operating it clearly didn't know how to grease the pivot shafts. You see that a lot with these newer operators—they just climb up in the cab, turn the key, and start pushing dirt, but they never do their daily walkarounds. And with a machine that heavy, if you ignore the track sag, you're going to wear out the front idlers so fast, and those things weigh a ton and cost an absolute fortune to replace. I spent like three hours just trying to hammer the master pin out of the track chain because it was practically welded in there with mud and rust. I finally had to get the oxy-acetylene torch out and heat it until it was glowing bright orange just to get it to budge... 

But, wait. I'm sorry, my brain is just all over the place. What did you just ask me? Something about focusing on a problem?

I don't think I feel hopeless... just stressed, you know? Like, I still enjoy working on stuff. It's the only thing that really makes sense right now. Actually, I was just looking at this older Peterbilt 379 the other day. Beautiful truck. It had the classic long hood and a Cat 3406E engine under it. Those engines are legendary, I mean, you can put a million miles on them and they just keep pulling. But this one had a weird issue with the Jake brake—the engine retarder. Usually, that means there's an issue with the solenoids under the valve cover, or maybe the wiring harness got brittle from the oil heat and cracked. But yeah, I'm just jumping out of my skin every time my phone rings. And at work, I'm snapping at the new apprentices, which isn't fair to them. They're just kids, really. One of them, Tommy, reminds me of my nephew. Always wearing his hat backwards and dropping his wrenches. But I'm making stupid mistakes in the repair bay, too—stuff I never would've messed up before. I'm not entirely sure what counts as that, honestly. I just get really wound up, you know? Like my stomach just drops when I see the mail truck pull up. Our carrier, Gary, he's this older guy who always wears those regulation postal shorts with the knee-high socks, even when it's freezing out. He's been doing our route for fifteen years, maybe more. He drives one of those old Grumman LLVs, those boxy little mail trucks? They have the old Iron Duke engine in them, which is practically indestructible if you keep the oil clean, but the chassis on those things rust out so bad around here because of all the road salt in the winter. I actually helped him patch a hole in his exhaust a few years back because he was getting fumes in the cab and the fleet mechanic was backed up. Good guy, Gary. But... wait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? I try to just sit and relax when I finally get home. I have this really great old La-Z-Boy recliner I bought at an estate sale a few years back. \n\nThe guy running the sale was moving down to Arizona, taking this massive Class A motorhome down there. It was a Winnebago, one of the big diesel pushers. Had a Cummins ISB 6.7-liter engine in it, which is a really solid block, but I had to warn him about the emissions systems on those. The DPF filters get completely choked up with soot if you just idle them or putt around town. You really have to get them up to highway speeds so the exhaust gets hot enough to run a proper regen cycle, or else you're looking at a massive repair bill and you're stuck in limp mode on the side of the highway. I even wrote down the specific fuel additive he needed on a piece of cardboard for him to keep in the glovebox... \n\nBut anyway, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? You kind of have to be in my line of work. \n\nLike, just this past Tuesday, we had this massive Caterpillar D8 dozer hauled into the yard. The undercarriage was completely shot. I mean, the track tension was all out of whack, and the sprockets were just chewed to pieces. The guy who was operating it clearly didn't know how to grease the pivot shafts. You see that a lot with these newer operators—they just climb up in the cab, turn the key, and start pushing dirt, but they never do their daily walkarounds. And with a machine that heavy, if you ignore the track sag, you're going to wear out the front idlers so fast, and those things weigh a ton and cost an absolute fortune to replace. I spent like three hours just trying to hammer the master pin out of the track chain because it was practically welded in there with mud and rust. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't think I'm unusually tired... I mean, I'm tired, but just the normal amount for what I do. It's a physical job. You're constantly climbing up and down, wrestling with heavy parts. Like, pulling a transmission out of an International WorkStar. You're talking about a piece of metal that weighs more than a small car. Even with a transmission jack, you're under there on your back, fighting gravity and trying to line up the splines on the input shaft with the clutch disc. If you're off by even a fraction of an inch, it won't seat, and you're just shoving hundreds of pounds of steel against a brick wall. And the bolts on the bell housing? Half the time they're seized up with road salt and grime, so you're putting your entire body weight into a breaker bar just to bust them loose. By the end of a shift doing that, your shoulders are burning and your hands are cramped up like claws. You definitely sleep hard after a day like that... 

Sorry, I'm rambling again. Did you ask if I was tired?

Ever since those foreclosure notices started coming in the mail, I feel like I'm just coming apart at the seams. I mean, I'm sitting in my truck crying before I even walk into the shop. And it's a Ford F-250, you know, the diesel one? I've had it for about eight years now. Rebuilt the transmission myself last summer because, well, you can't trust just anyone with those older models, and the parts are getting so expensive anyway. But yeah, I'm just jumping out of my skin every time my phone rings. And at work, I'm snapping at the new apprentices, which isn't fair to them. They're just kids, really. One of them, Tommy, reminds me of my nephew. Always wearing his hat backwards and dropping his wrenches. But I'm making stupid mistakes in the repair bay, too—stuff I never would've messed up before. Just completely losing my focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or have episodes of intense worry or panic, especially related to the stress with the foreclosure?\n\nPatient: I don't really know about... like, actual panic episodes? I'm not entirely sure what counts as that, honestly. I just get really wound up, you know? Like my stomach just drops when I see the mail truck pull up. Our carrier, Gary, he's this older guy who always wears those regulation postal shorts with the knee-high socks, even when it's freezing out. He's been doing our route for fifteen years, maybe more. He drives one of those old Grumman LLVs, those boxy little mail trucks? They have the old Iron Duke engine in them, which is practically indestructible if you keep the oil clean, but the chassis on those things rust out so bad around here because of all the road salt in the winter. I actually helped him patch a hole in his exhaust a few years back because he was getting fumes in the cab and the fleet mechanic was backed up. Good guy, Gary. But... wait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about the bank?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel physically restless or on edge, like you can't relax or you're constantly watching for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: I don't think so? I mean, I wouldn't say I feel physically restless or like I'm constantly looking over my shoulder for something bad to happen. I don't really have that. I try to just sit and relax when I finally get home. I have this really great old La-Z-Boy recliner I bought at an estate sale a few years back. \n\nThe guy running the sale was moving down to Arizona, taking this massive Class A motorhome down there. It was a Winnebago, one of the big diesel pushers. Had a Cummins ISB 6.7-liter engine in it, which is a really solid block, but I had to warn him about the emissions systems on those. The DPF filters get completely choked up with soot if you just idle them or putt around town. You really have to get them up to highway speeds so the exhaust gets hot enough to run a proper regen cycle, or else you're looking at a massive repair bill and you're stuck in limp mode on the side of the highway. I even wrote down the specific fuel additive he needed on a piece of cardboard for him to keep in the glovebox... \n\nBut anyway, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about relaxing?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason or specific problem to focus on?\n\nPatient: Not really... I don't just worry about random stuff for no reason. It's really just the bank and the house that's eating me alive right now. Normally, I'm pretty good at just focusing on what's in front of me and not stressing over things that aren't there. You kind of have to be in my line of work. \n\nLike, just this past Tuesday, we had this massive Caterpillar D8 dozer hauled into the yard. The undercarriage was completely shot. I mean, the track tension was all out of whack, and the sprockets were just chewed to pieces. The guy who was operating it clearly didn't know how to grease the pivot shafts. You see that a lot with these newer operators—they just climb up in the cab, turn the key, and start pushing dirt, but they never do their daily walkarounds. And with a machine that heavy, if you ignore the track sag, you're going to wear out the front idlers so fast, and those things weigh a ton and cost an absolute fortune to replace. I spent like three hours just trying to hammer the master pin out of the track chain because it was practically welded in there with mud and rust. I finally had to get the oxy-acetylene torch out and heat it until it was glowing bright orange just to get it to budge... \n\nBut, wait. I'm sorry, my brain is just all over the place. But this one had a weird issue with the Jake brake—the engine retarder. The driver said it was only engaging on three cylinders instead of all six. Usually, that means there's an issue with the solenoids under the valve cover, or maybe the wiring harness got brittle from the oil heat and cracked. You have to pull the whole top end off just to test the ohms on those solenoids, and getting the gasket to seat right when you put it back together is a real pain if you don't clean the mating surface perfectly. Like, I still enjoy working on stuff. It's the only thing that really makes sense right now. Actually, I was just looking at this older Peterbilt 379 the other day. Beautiful truck. It had the classic long hood and a Cat 3406E engine under it. Those engines are legendary, I mean, you can put a million miles on them and they just keep pulling. But this one had a weird issue with the Jake brake—the engine retarder. The driver said it was only engaging on three cylinders instead of all six. Usually, that means there's an issue with the solenoids under the valve cover, or maybe the wiring harness got brittle from the oil heat and cracked. You have to pull the whole top end off just to test the ohms on those solenoids, and getting the gasket to seat right when you put it back together is a real pain if you don't clean the mating surface perfectly. I actually keep a special set of brass scrapers just for that so I don't gouge the aluminum housing... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, I did it again, didn't I? What was the question? Something about feeling down?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you’re not doing much, you feel unusually tired or like you don’t have much energy?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'm unusually tired... I mean, I'm tired, but just the normal amount for what I do. It's a physical job. You're constantly climbing up and down, wrestling with heavy parts. Like, pulling a transmission out of an International WorkStar. You're talking about a piece of metal that weighs more than a small car. Even with a transmission jack, you're under there on your back, fighting gravity and trying to line up the splines on the input shaft with the clutch disc. If you're off by even a fraction of an inch, it won't seat, and you're just shoving hundreds of pounds of steel against a brick wall. And the bolts on the bell housing? Half the time they're seized up with road salt and grime, so you're putting your entire body weight into a breaker bar just to bust them loose. By the end of a shift doing that, your shoulders are burning and your hands are cramped up like claws. You definitely sleep hard after a day like that... \n\nSorry, I'm rambling again. Did you ask if I was tired?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any trouble concentrating or staying focused, even on tasks you normally find straightforward, like working on engines or diagnosing mechanical issues?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / heavy_vehicle_or_mobile_equipment_service_technician_or_mechanic / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I haven't really had any trouble concentrating. I mean, when I'm under a hood, I'm locked in. That's the one place where everything still makes sense, you know? Like, just yesterday, I was diagnosing a rough idle on this big John Deere loader. The owner thought it was bad fuel, but I knew right away it was something else. You could hear this slight hesitation, almost like a misfire, but not quite. 

I hooked up the diagnostic laptop, but honestly, sometimes those codes just send you on a wild goose chase. You have to trust your ears and your hands. I started checking the fuel injectors, and sure enough, the wiring harness going to the number four injector was rubbing against the engine block. The insulation was worn right through, so every time the engine vibrated, it was shorting out against the metal. It's such a simple thing, but if you don't know where to look, you could spend days tearing the fuel system apart. I just wrapped it in some high-temp wire loom and zip-tied it out of the way. Took me maybe twenty minutes, but the guy was thrilled. He thought he was going to have to replace the whole ECM. 

Anyway... wait, what was the question? Did you ask about my concentration?

It's such a simple thing, but if you don't know where to look, you could spend days tearing the fuel system apart. I just wrapped it in some high-temp wire loom and zip-tied it out of the way. Took me maybe twenty minutes, but the guy was thrilled. He thought he was going to have to replace the whole ECM. \n\nAnyway... wait, what was the question? Did you ask about my concentration?\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the last few weeks, have you felt down or lost interest in things you usually care about, even when you're not thinking about the foreclosure?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like working on trucks or helping people like Gary with their vehicles?\n\nPatient: I don't think I feel hopeless... just stressed, you know? Like, I still enjoy working on stuff. It's the only thing that really makes sense right now. Actually, I was just looking at this older Peterbilt 379 the other day. Beautiful truck. It had the classic long hood and a Cat 3406E engine under it. Those engines are legendary, I mean, you can put a million miles on them and they just keep pulling. But this one had a weird issue with the Jake brake—the engine retarder. The driver said it was only engaging on three cylinders instead of all six. Usually, that means there's an issue with the solenoids under the valve cover, or maybe the wiring harness got brittle from the oil heat and cracked. You have to pull the whole top end off just to test the ohms on those solenoids, and getting the gasket to seat right when you put it back together is a real pain if you don't clean the mating surface perfectly. I actually keep a special set of brass scrapers just for that so I don't gouge the aluminum housing... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, I did it again, didn't I? What was the question? Something about feeling down?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you’re not doing much, you feel unusually tired or like you don’t have much energy?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'm unusually tired... I mean, I'm tired, but just the normal amount for what I do. It's a physical job. You're constantly climbing up and down, wrestling with heavy parts. Like, pulling a transmission out of an International WorkStar. You're talking about a piece of metal that weighs more than a small car. Even with a transmission jack, you're under there on your back, fighting gravity and trying to line up the splines on the input shaft with the clutch disc. If you're off by even a fraction of an inch, it won't seat, and you're just shoving hundreds of pounds of steel against a brick wall. And the bolts on the bell housing? Half the time they're seized up with road salt and grime, so you're putting your entire body weight into a breaker bar just to bust them loose. You could hear this slight hesitation, almost like a misfire, but not quite. \n\nI hooked up the diagnostic laptop, but honestly, sometimes those codes just send you on a wild goose chase. The insulation was worn right through, so every time the engine vibrated, it was shorting out against the metal. It's such a simple thing, but if you don't know where to look, you could spend days tearing the fuel system apart. I just wrapped it in some high-temp wire loom and zip-tied it out of the way. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't think I've really felt down, no. Like I said, working on the trucks is keeping me going. It's just the stress of the house, you know? But otherwise, I still care about my work. Actually, I was helping Gary out just this morning with his Freightliner. He's got this Cascadia, one of the newer ones with the DD15 engine. They're decent motors, but the emission systems on them are an absolute nightmare. 

He was getting a derate code for the DEF system—diesel exhaust fluid. The computer thought the tank was empty even though he just filled it. Usually, that means the DEF header unit is shot. It's this long probe that goes down into the tank to measure the level, the temperature, and the quality of the fluid all at once. The problem is, the coolant lines run through it to keep the DEF from freezing in the winter, and if those lines leak inside the tank, it contaminates the whole system. You have to drain the tank, flush the lines, replace the header, and sometimes even swap out the dosing valve if the crystalized DEF clogged it up. It's a messy, awful job, and the fluid smells like stale ammonia. You get it on your hands and it just dries out your skin instantly...

Sorry. I'm doing it again. Did you ask if I was feeling down?

Rebuilt the transmission myself last summer because, well, you can't trust just anyone with those older models, and the parts are getting so expensive anyway. But yeah, I'm just jumping out of my skin every time my phone rings. And at work, I'm snapping at the new apprentices, which isn't fair to them. They're just kids, really. One of them, Tommy, reminds me of my nephew. Always wearing his hat backwards and dropping his wrenches. But I'm making stupid mistakes in the repair bay, too—stuff I never would've messed up before. Just completely losing my focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or have episodes of intense worry or panic, especially related to the stress with the foreclosure?\n\nPatient: I don't really know about... like, actual panic episodes? I'm not entirely sure what counts as that, honestly. I just get really wound up, you know? Like my stomach just drops when I see the mail truck pull up. Our carrier, Gary, he's this older guy who always wears those regulation postal shorts with the knee-high socks, even when it's freezing out. He's been doing our route for fifteen years, maybe more. He drives one of those old Grumman LLVs, those boxy little mail trucks? They have the old Iron Duke engine in them, which is practically indestructible if you keep the oil clean, but the chassis on those things rust out so bad around here because of all the road salt in the winter. I actually helped him patch a hole in his exhaust a few years back because he was getting fumes in the cab and the fleet mechanic was backed up. Good guy, Gary. But... wait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about the bank?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel physically restless or on edge, like you can't relax or you're constantly watching for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: I don't think so? I mean, I wouldn't say I feel physically restless or like I'm constantly looking over my shoulder for something bad to happen. I don't really have that. I try to just sit and relax when I finally get home. I have this really great old La-Z-Boy recliner I bought at an estate sale a few years back. \n\nThe guy running the sale was moving down to Arizona, taking this massive Class A motorhome down there. It was a Winnebago, one of the big diesel pushers. Had a Cummins ISB 6.7-liter engine in it, which is a really solid block, but I had to warn him about the emissions systems on those. The DPF filters get completely choked up with soot if you just idle them or putt around town. You really have to get them up to highway speeds so the exhaust gets hot enough to run a proper regen cycle, or else you're looking at a massive repair bill and you're stuck in limp mode on the side of the highway. I even wrote down the specific fuel additive he needed on a piece of cardboard for him to keep in the glovebox... \n\nBut anyway, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about relaxing?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason or specific problem to focus on?\n\nPatient: Not really... I don't just worry about random stuff for no reason. It's really just the bank and the house that's eating me alive right now. Normally, I'm pretty good at just focusing on what's in front of me and not stressing over things that aren't there. You kind of have to be in my line of work. \n\nLike, just this past Tuesday, we had this massive Caterpillar D8 dozer hauled into the yard. The undercarriage was completely shot. I mean, the track tension was all out of whack, and the sprockets were just chewed to pieces. The guy who was operating it clearly didn't know how to grease the pivot shafts. You see that a lot with these newer operators—they just climb up in the cab, turn the key, and start pushing dirt, but they never do their daily walkarounds. And with a machine that heavy, if you ignore the track sag, you're going to wear out the front idlers so fast, and those things weigh a ton and cost an absolute fortune to replace. I spent like three hours just trying to hammer the master pin out of the track chain because it was practically welded in there with mud and rust. I finally had to get the oxy-acetylene torch out and heat it until it was glowing bright orange just to get it to budge... \n\nBut, wait. I'm sorry, my brain is just all over the place. What did you just ask me? Something about focusing on a problem?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like working on trucks or helping people like Gary with their vehicles?\n\nPatient: I don't think I feel hopeless... just stressed, you know? Like, I still enjoy working on stuff. It's the only thing that really makes sense right now. Actually, I was just looking at this older Peterbilt 379 the other day. Beautiful truck. It had the classic long hood and a Cat 3406E engine under it. Those engines are legendary, I mean, you can put a million miles on them and they just keep pulling. But this one had a weird issue with the Jake brake—the engine retarder. The driver said it was only engaging on three cylinders instead of all six. Usually, that means there's an issue with the solenoids under the valve cover, or maybe the wiring harness got brittle from the oil heat and cracked. You have to pull the whole top end off just to test the ohms on those solenoids, and getting the gasket to seat right when you put it back together is a real pain if you don't clean the mating surface perfectly. I actually keep a special set of brass scrapers just for that so I don't gouge the aluminum housing... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, I did it again, didn't I? What was the question? Something about feeling down?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you’re not doing much, you feel unusually tired or like you don’t have much energy?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'm unusually tired... I mean, I'm tired, but just the normal amount for what I do. It's a physical job. You're constantly climbing up and down, wrestling with heavy parts. Like, pulling a transmission out of an International WorkStar. You're talking about a piece of metal that weighs more than a small car. Even with a transmission jack, you're under there on your back, fighting gravity and trying to line up the splines on the input shaft with the clutch disc. If you're off by even a fraction of an inch, it won't seat, and you're just shoving hundreds of pounds of steel against a brick wall. And the bolts on the bell housing? Half the time they're seized up with road salt and grime, so you're putting your entire body weight into a breaker bar just to bust them loose. By the end of a shift doing that, your shoulders are burning and your hands are cramped up like claws. You definitely sleep hard after a day like that... \n\nSorry, I'm rambling again. Did you ask if I was tired?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or staying focused, even on tasks you normally find straightforward, like working on engines or diagnosing mechanical issues?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any trouble concentrating. I mean, when I'm under a hood, I'm locked in. That's the one place where everything still makes sense, you know? Like, just yesterday, I was diagnosing a rough idle on this big John Deere loader. The owner thought it was bad fuel, but I knew right away it was something else. You could hear this slight hesitation, almost like a misfire, but not quite. \n\nI hooked up the diagnostic laptop, but honestly, sometimes those codes just send you on a wild goose chase. You have to trust your ears and your hands. I started checking the fuel injectors, and sure enough, the wiring harness going to the number four injector was rubbing against the engine block. The insulation was worn right through, so every time the engine vibrated, it was shorting out against the metal. It's such a simple thing, but if you don't know where to look, you could spend days tearing the fuel system apart. I just wrapped it in some high-temp wire loom and zip-tied it out of the way. Took me maybe twenty minutes, but the guy was thrilled. He thought he was going to have to replace the whole ECM. \n\nAnyway... wait, what was the question? Did you ask about my concentration?\n\nDoctor: Over the last few weeks, have you felt down or lost interest in things you usually care about, even when you're not thinking about the foreclosure?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've really felt down, no. Like I said, working on the trucks is keeping me going. It's just the stress of the house, you know? But otherwise, I still care about my work. Actually, I was helping Gary out just this morning with his Freightliner. He's got this Cascadia, one of the newer ones with the DD15 engine. They're decent motors, but the emission systems on them are an absolute nightmare. \n\nHe was getting a derate code for the DEF system—diesel exhaust fluid. The computer thought the tank was empty even though he just filled it. Usually, that means the DEF header unit is shot. It's this long probe that goes down into the tank to measure the level, the temperature, and the quality of the fluid all at once. The problem is, the coolant lines run through it to keep the DEF from freezing in the winter, and if those lines leak inside the tank, it contaminates the whole system. You have to drain the tank, flush the lines, replace the header, and sometimes even swap out the dosing valve if the crystalized DEF clogged it up. It's a messy, awful job, and the fluid smells like stale ammonia. You get it on your hands and it just dries out your skin instantly...\n\nSorry. I'm doing it again. Did you ask if I was feeling down?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or lost interest in things outside of work, even when you’re not thinking about the foreclosure or money issues?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I don't have a ton of hobbies outside of the shop anyway, but I still care about the things I normally do. Like, I've got this old Ford F-250 sitting in my driveway. It's a 7.3 Powerstroke—the good year, before they ruined them with all that extra garbage. I was actually out there messing with it this past weekend. \n\nThe high-pressure oil pump is starting to weep a little bit. You can always tell because you start smelling oil and it pools down in the valley pan of the engine block. It's a classic issue with those 7.3s. The O-rings on the HPOP lines just get brittle from all the extreme heat cycles, and eventually, they let go. If you ignore it, you lose pressure and the truck won't even start when it's hot. It's kind of a pain to fix, though. You have to pull the whole fuel bowl assembly just to reach the fittings, and it's a really tight squeeze back there against the firewall. I bought the rebuild kit a while ago, the one with the upgraded Viton seals that are supposed to handle the heat better, and it's just sitting on my workbench. I usually like doing that kind of stuff on a Sunday, just put the radio on and wrench on my own stuff for a change without a service manager breathing down my neck... \n\nWait, I lost my train of thought. What was the question again? Something about my days off?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Well, I don't know if I'd say I've lost interest, but... things have definitely changed. Like, at work, they actually took me out of the high-risk repair bay. Which, I mean, I get it, I've been a little distracted with the whole house situation, but it still stings, you know? That's where all the heavy diagnostic stuff happens, the really complicated tear-downs. Like last month, we had this Caterpillar C15 come in, and the guy had somehow managed to drop a valve. The piston just hammered it into the cylinder head. It was a complete mess. You have to pull the whole head, check the liner for scoring, make sure the connecting rod isn't bent... it's a massive job. You need the overhead crane just to lift the head off because it weighs like 400 pounds. I usually love getting into those deep engine rebuilds. You have to be so precise with the torque specs and the overhead valve adjustments. If you're off by even a few thousandths of an inch on the injector height, the engine will run like garbage and blow black smoke everywhere. 

But yeah, they moved me to lighter duty for now. And outside of work... I guess I just haven't been doing much. I used to volunteer at church, go to these fitness classes, and I'd always go to the truck-club outings. We used to meet up at the diner on Route 9, a bunch of us with older rigs, just talking shop and drinking terrible coffee. There's this one guy, Stan, who has a beautiful old Peterbilt 359, the classic long-hood with the dual stacks. He actually swapped a mechanical Cummins Big Cam into it, which is just... I mean, the sound of that engine under load is incredible. It doesn't have all the computer nonsense, just pure mechanical fuel injection. You actually have to know how to drive it, how to float the gears without grinding...

Wait, sorry. I'm drifting again. What did you ask me? About my days off?

I'm not entirely sure what counts as that, honestly. I just get really wound up, you know? Like my stomach just drops when I see the mail truck pull up. Our carrier, Gary, he's this older guy who always wears those regulation postal shorts with the knee-high socks, even when it's freezing out. He's been doing our route for fifteen years, maybe more. He drives one of those old Grumman LLVs, those boxy little mail trucks? They have the old Iron Duke engine in them, which is practically indestructible if you keep the oil clean, but the chassis on those things rust out so bad around here because of all the road salt in the winter. I actually helped him patch a hole in his exhaust a few years back because he was getting fumes in the cab and the fleet mechanic was backed up. Good guy, Gary. But... wait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about the bank?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel physically restless or on edge, like you can't relax or you're constantly watching for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: I don't think so? I mean, I wouldn't say I feel physically restless or like I'm constantly looking over my shoulder for something bad to happen. I don't really have that. I try to just sit and relax when I finally get home. I have this really great old La-Z-Boy recliner I bought at an estate sale a few years back. \n\nThe guy running the sale was moving down to Arizona, taking this massive Class A motorhome down there. It was a Winnebago, one of the big diesel pushers. Had a Cummins ISB 6.7-liter engine in it, which is a really solid block, but I had to warn him about the emissions systems on those. The DPF filters get completely choked up with soot if you just idle them or putt around town. You really have to get them up to highway speeds so the exhaust gets hot enough to run a proper regen cycle, or else you're looking at a massive repair bill and you're stuck in limp mode on the side of the highway. I even wrote down the specific fuel additive he needed on a piece of cardboard for him to keep in the glovebox... \n\nBut anyway, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about relaxing?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason or specific problem to focus on?\n\nPatient: Not really... I don't just worry about random stuff for no reason. It's really just the bank and the house that's eating me alive right now. Normally, I'm pretty good at just focusing on what's in front of me and not stressing over things that aren't there. You kind of have to be in my line of work. \n\nLike, just this past Tuesday, we had this massive Caterpillar D8 dozer hauled into the yard. The undercarriage was completely shot. I mean, the track tension was all out of whack, and the sprockets were just chewed to pieces. The guy who was operating it clearly didn't know how to grease the pivot shafts. You see that a lot with these newer operators—they just climb up in the cab, turn the key, and start pushing dirt, but they never do their daily walkarounds. And with a machine that heavy, if you ignore the track sag, you're going to wear out the front idlers so fast, and those things weigh a ton and cost an absolute fortune to replace. I spent like three hours just trying to hammer the master pin out of the track chain because it was practically welded in there with mud and rust. I finally had to get the oxy-acetylene torch out and heat it until it was glowing bright orange just to get it to budge... \n\nBut, wait. I'm sorry, my brain is just all over the place. What did you just ask me? Something about focusing on a problem?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like working on trucks or helping people like Gary with their vehicles?\n\nPatient: I don't think I feel hopeless... just stressed, you know? Like, I still enjoy working on stuff. It's the only thing that really makes sense right now. Actually, I was just looking at this older Peterbilt 379 the other day. Beautiful truck. It had the classic long hood and a Cat 3406E engine under it. Those engines are legendary, I mean, you can put a million miles on them and they just keep pulling. But this one had a weird issue with the Jake brake—the engine retarder. The driver said it was only engaging on three cylinders instead of all six. Usually, that means there's an issue with the solenoids under the valve cover, or maybe the wiring harness got brittle from the oil heat and cracked. You have to pull the whole top end off just to test the ohms on those solenoids, and getting the gasket to seat right when you put it back together is a real pain if you don't clean the mating surface perfectly. I actually keep a special set of brass scrapers just for that so I don't gouge the aluminum housing... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, I did it again, didn't I? What was the question? Something about feeling down?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you’re not doing much, you feel unusually tired or like you don’t have much energy?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'm unusually tired... I mean, I'm tired, but just the normal amount for what I do. It's a physical job. You're constantly climbing up and down, wrestling with heavy parts. Like, pulling a transmission out of an International WorkStar. You're talking about a piece of metal that weighs more than a small car. Even with a transmission jack, you're under there on your back, fighting gravity and trying to line up the splines on the input shaft with the clutch disc. If you're off by even a fraction of an inch, it won't seat, and you're just shoving hundreds of pounds of steel against a brick wall. And the bolts on the bell housing? Half the time they're seized up with road salt and grime, so you're putting your entire body weight into a breaker bar just to bust them loose. By the end of a shift doing that, your shoulders are burning and your hands are cramped up like claws. You definitely sleep hard after a day like that... \n\nSorry, I'm rambling again. Did you ask if I was tired?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or staying focused, even on tasks you normally find straightforward, like working on engines or diagnosing mechanical issues?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any trouble concentrating. I mean, when I'm under a hood, I'm locked in. That's the one place where everything still makes sense, you know? Like, just yesterday, I was diagnosing a rough idle on this big John Deere loader. The owner thought it was bad fuel, but I knew right away it was something else. You could hear this slight hesitation, almost like a misfire, but not quite. \n\nI hooked up the diagnostic laptop, but honestly, sometimes those codes just send you on a wild goose chase. You have to trust your ears and your hands. I started checking the fuel injectors, and sure enough, the wiring harness going to the number four injector was rubbing against the engine block. The insulation was worn right through, so every time the engine vibrated, it was shorting out against the metal. It's such a simple thing, but if you don't know where to look, you could spend days tearing the fuel system apart. I just wrapped it in some high-temp wire loom and zip-tied it out of the way. Took me maybe twenty minutes, but the guy was thrilled. He thought he was going to have to replace the whole ECM. \n\nAnyway... wait, what was the question? Did you ask about my concentration?\n\nDoctor: Over the last few weeks, have you felt down or lost interest in things you usually care about, even when you're not thinking about the foreclosure?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've really felt down, no. Like I said, working on the trucks is keeping me going. It's just the stress of the house, you know? But otherwise, I still care about my work. Actually, I was helping Gary out just this morning with his Freightliner. He's got this Cascadia, one of the newer ones with the DD15 engine. They're decent motors, but the emission systems on them are an absolute nightmare. \n\nHe was getting a derate code for the DEF system—diesel exhaust fluid. The computer thought the tank was empty even though he just filled it. Usually, that means the DEF header unit is shot. It's this long probe that goes down into the tank to measure the level, the temperature, and the quality of the fluid all at once. The problem is, the coolant lines run through it to keep the DEF from freezing in the winter, and if those lines leak inside the tank, it contaminates the whole system. You have to drain the tank, flush the lines, replace the header, and sometimes even swap out the dosing valve if the crystalized DEF clogged it up. It's a messy, awful job, and the fluid smells like stale ammonia. You get it on your hands and it just dries out your skin instantly...\n\nSorry. I'm doing it again. Did you ask if I was feeling down?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or lost interest in things outside of work, even when you’re not thinking about the foreclosure or money issues?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I don't have a ton of hobbies outside of the shop anyway, but I still care about the things I normally do. Like, I've got this old Ford F-250 sitting in my driveway. It's a 7.3 Powerstroke—the good year, before they ruined them with all that extra garbage. I was actually out there messing with it this past weekend. \n\nThe high-pressure oil pump is starting to weep a little bit. You can always tell because you start smelling oil and it pools down in the valley pan of the engine block. It's a classic issue with those 7.3s. The O-rings on the HPOP lines just get brittle from all the extreme heat cycles, and eventually, they let go. If you ignore it, you lose pressure and the truck won't even start when it's hot. It's kind of a pain to fix, though. You have to pull the whole fuel bowl assembly just to reach the fittings, and it's a really tight squeeze back there against the firewall. I bought the rebuild kit a while ago, the one with the upgraded Viton seals that are supposed to handle the heat better, and it's just sitting on my workbench. I usually like doing that kind of stuff on a Sunday, just put the radio on and wrench on my own stuff for a change without a service manager breathing down my neck... \n\nWait, I lost my train of thought. What was the question again? Something about my days off?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or lost interest in things you normally enjoy doing on your days off, like spending time with family, hobbies, or relaxing activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if I'd say I've lost interest, but... things have definitely changed. Like, at work, they actually took me out of the high-risk repair bay. Which, I mean, I get it, I've been a little distracted with the whole house situation, but it still stings, you know? That's where all the heavy diagnostic stuff happens, the really complicated tear-downs. Like last month, we had this Caterpillar C15 come in, and the guy had somehow managed to drop a valve. The piston just hammered it into the cylinder head. It was a complete mess. You have to pull the whole head, check the liner for scoring, make sure the connecting rod isn't bent... it's a massive job. You need the overhead crane just to lift the head off because it weighs like 400 pounds. I usually love getting into those deep engine rebuilds. You have to be so precise with the torque specs and the overhead valve adjustments. If you're off by even a few thousandths of an inch on the injector height, the engine will run like garbage and blow black smoke everywhere. \n\nBut yeah, they moved me to lighter duty for now. And outside of work... I guess I just haven't been doing much. I used to volunteer at church, go to these fitness classes, and I'd always go to the truck-club outings. We used to meet up at the diner on Route 9, a bunch of us with older rigs, just talking shop and drinking terrible coffee. There's this one guy, Stan, who has a beautiful old Peterbilt 359, the classic long-hood with the dual stacks. He actually swapped a mechanical Cummins Big Cam into it, which is just... I mean, the sound of that engine under load is incredible. It doesn't have all the computer nonsense, just pure mechanical fuel injection. You actually have to know how to drive it, how to float the gears without grinding...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm drifting again. What did you ask me? About my days off?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I haven't really noticed my mind going blank or anything like that. I mean, I still make decisions fine, mostly. Like at the shop, even on light duty, you still have to decide the best way to tackle a job. Yesterday I was doing a brake job on this municipal dump truck—an International WorkStar. The rear drums on those things are massive, and the spring brakes can be really dangerous if you don't cage them right before you pull the chamber off. There's a heavy-duty coil spring inside that chamber holding back a couple thousand pounds of force, and if you just unbolt the clamp band without caging it with the T-bolt, that spring will blow the whole housing apart. People have gotten killed by it. It's crazy to think about, really. Just one little bolt is all that stands between you and a trip to the ER. You always have to make sure the threads on the T-bolt aren't stripped, too. I've seen guys try to use rusty ones they found in the bottom of their toolbox, and I always yell at them. It's just not worth the risk for a five-dollar part...

Sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Did you ask about making decisions?

But yeah, I'm just jumping out of my skin every time my phone rings. And at work, I'm snapping at the new apprentices, which isn't fair to them. They're just kids, really. One of them, Tommy, reminds me of my nephew. Always wearing his hat backwards and dropping his wrenches. But I'm making stupid mistakes in the repair bay, too—stuff I never would've messed up before. Just completely losing my focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or have episodes of intense worry or panic, especially related to the stress with the foreclosure?\n\nPatient: I don't really know about... like, actual panic episodes? I'm not entirely sure what counts as that, honestly. I just get really wound up, you know? Like my stomach just drops when I see the mail truck pull up. Our carrier, Gary, he's this older guy who always wears those regulation postal shorts with the knee-high socks, even when it's freezing out. He's been doing our route for fifteen years, maybe more. He drives one of those old Grumman LLVs, those boxy little mail trucks? They have the old Iron Duke engine in them, which is practically indestructible if you keep the oil clean, but the chassis on those things rust out so bad around here because of all the road salt in the winter. I actually helped him patch a hole in his exhaust a few years back because he was getting fumes in the cab and the fleet mechanic was backed up. Good guy, Gary. But... wait, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about the bank?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel physically restless or on edge, like you can't relax or you're constantly watching for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: I don't think so? I mean, I wouldn't say I feel physically restless or like I'm constantly looking over my shoulder for something bad to happen. I don't really have that. I try to just sit and relax when I finally get home. I have this really great old La-Z-Boy recliner I bought at an estate sale a few years back. \n\nThe guy running the sale was moving down to Arizona, taking this massive Class A motorhome down there. It was a Winnebago, one of the big diesel pushers. Had a Cummins ISB 6.7-liter engine in it, which is a really solid block, but I had to warn him about the emissions systems on those. The DPF filters get completely choked up with soot if you just idle them or putt around town. You really have to get them up to highway speeds so the exhaust gets hot enough to run a proper regen cycle, or else you're looking at a massive repair bill and you're stuck in limp mode on the side of the highway. I even wrote down the specific fuel additive he needed on a piece of cardboard for him to keep in the glovebox... \n\nBut anyway, I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about relaxing?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different things, even when there isn’t a clear reason or specific problem to focus on?\n\nPatient: Not really... I don't just worry about random stuff for no reason. It's really just the bank and the house that's eating me alive right now. Normally, I'm pretty good at just focusing on what's in front of me and not stressing over things that aren't there. You kind of have to be in my line of work. \n\nLike, just this past Tuesday, we had this massive Caterpillar D8 dozer hauled into the yard. The undercarriage was completely shot. I mean, the track tension was all out of whack, and the sprockets were just chewed to pieces. The guy who was operating it clearly didn't know how to grease the pivot shafts. You see that a lot with these newer operators—they just climb up in the cab, turn the key, and start pushing dirt, but they never do their daily walkarounds. And with a machine that heavy, if you ignore the track sag, you're going to wear out the front idlers so fast, and those things weigh a ton and cost an absolute fortune to replace. I spent like three hours just trying to hammer the master pin out of the track chain because it was practically welded in there with mud and rust. I finally had to get the oxy-acetylene torch out and heat it until it was glowing bright orange just to get it to budge... \n\nBut, wait. I'm sorry, my brain is just all over the place. What did you just ask me? Something about focusing on a problem?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, like working on trucks or helping people like Gary with their vehicles?\n\nPatient: I don't think I feel hopeless... just stressed, you know? Like, I still enjoy working on stuff. It's the only thing that really makes sense right now. Actually, I was just looking at this older Peterbilt 379 the other day. Beautiful truck. It had the classic long hood and a Cat 3406E engine under it. Those engines are legendary, I mean, you can put a million miles on them and they just keep pulling. But this one had a weird issue with the Jake brake—the engine retarder. The driver said it was only engaging on three cylinders instead of all six. Usually, that means there's an issue with the solenoids under the valve cover, or maybe the wiring harness got brittle from the oil heat and cracked. You have to pull the whole top end off just to test the ohms on those solenoids, and getting the gasket to seat right when you put it back together is a real pain if you don't clean the mating surface perfectly. I actually keep a special set of brass scrapers just for that so I don't gouge the aluminum housing... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, I did it again, didn't I? What was the question? Something about feeling down?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you’re not doing much, you feel unusually tired or like you don’t have much energy?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'm unusually tired... I mean, I'm tired, but just the normal amount for what I do. It's a physical job. You're constantly climbing up and down, wrestling with heavy parts. Like, pulling a transmission out of an International WorkStar. You're talking about a piece of metal that weighs more than a small car. Even with a transmission jack, you're under there on your back, fighting gravity and trying to line up the splines on the input shaft with the clutch disc. If you're off by even a fraction of an inch, it won't seat, and you're just shoving hundreds of pounds of steel against a brick wall. And the bolts on the bell housing? Half the time they're seized up with road salt and grime, so you're putting your entire body weight into a breaker bar just to bust them loose. By the end of a shift doing that, your shoulders are burning and your hands are cramped up like claws. You definitely sleep hard after a day like that... \n\nSorry, I'm rambling again. Did you ask if I was tired?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or staying focused, even on tasks you normally find straightforward, like working on engines or diagnosing mechanical issues?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any trouble concentrating. I mean, when I'm under a hood, I'm locked in. That's the one place where everything still makes sense, you know? Like, just yesterday, I was diagnosing a rough idle on this big John Deere loader. The owner thought it was bad fuel, but I knew right away it was something else. You could hear this slight hesitation, almost like a misfire, but not quite. \n\nI hooked up the diagnostic laptop, but honestly, sometimes those codes just send you on a wild goose chase. You have to trust your ears and your hands. I started checking the fuel injectors, and sure enough, the wiring harness going to the number four injector was rubbing against the engine block. The insulation was worn right through, so every time the engine vibrated, it was shorting out against the metal. It's such a simple thing, but if you don't know where to look, you could spend days tearing the fuel system apart. I just wrapped it in some high-temp wire loom and zip-tied it out of the way. Took me maybe twenty minutes, but the guy was thrilled. He thought he was going to have to replace the whole ECM. \n\nAnyway... wait, what was the question? Did you ask about my concentration?\n\nDoctor: Over the last few weeks, have you felt down or lost interest in things you usually care about, even when you're not thinking about the foreclosure?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've really felt down, no. Like I said, working on the trucks is keeping me going. It's just the stress of the house, you know? But otherwise, I still care about my work. Actually, I was helping Gary out just this morning with his Freightliner. He's got this Cascadia, one of the newer ones with the DD15 engine. They're decent motors, but the emission systems on them are an absolute nightmare. \n\nHe was getting a derate code for the DEF system—diesel exhaust fluid. The computer thought the tank was empty even though he just filled it. Usually, that means the DEF header unit is shot. It's this long probe that goes down into the tank to measure the level, the temperature, and the quality of the fluid all at once. The problem is, the coolant lines run through it to keep the DEF from freezing in the winter, and if those lines leak inside the tank, it contaminates the whole system. You have to drain the tank, flush the lines, replace the header, and sometimes even swap out the dosing valve if the crystalized DEF clogged it up. It's a messy, awful job, and the fluid smells like stale ammonia. You get it on your hands and it just dries out your skin instantly...\n\nSorry. I'm doing it again. Did you ask if I was feeling down?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or lost interest in things outside of work, even when you’re not thinking about the foreclosure or money issues?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. I mean, I don't have a ton of hobbies outside of the shop anyway, but I still care about the things I normally do. Like, I've got this old Ford F-250 sitting in my driveway. It's a 7.3 Powerstroke—the good year, before they ruined them with all that extra garbage. I was actually out there messing with it this past weekend. \n\nThe high-pressure oil pump is starting to weep a little bit. You can always tell because you start smelling oil and it pools down in the valley pan of the engine block. It's a classic issue with those 7.3s. The O-rings on the HPOP lines just get brittle from all the extreme heat cycles, and eventually, they let go. If you ignore it, you lose pressure and the truck won't even start when it's hot. It's kind of a pain to fix, though. You have to pull the whole fuel bowl assembly just to reach the fittings, and it's a really tight squeeze back there against the firewall. I bought the rebuild kit a while ago, the one with the upgraded Viton seals that are supposed to handle the heat better, and it's just sitting on my workbench. I usually like doing that kind of stuff on a Sunday, just put the radio on and wrench on my own stuff for a change without a service manager breathing down my neck... \n\nWait, I lost my train of thought. What was the question again? Something about my days off?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or lost interest in things you normally enjoy doing on your days off, like spending time with family, hobbies, or relaxing activities?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if I'd say I've lost interest, but... things have definitely changed. Like, at work, they actually took me out of the high-risk repair bay. Which, I mean, I get it, I've been a little distracted with the whole house situation, but it still stings, you know? That's where all the heavy diagnostic stuff happens, the really complicated tear-downs. Like last month, we had this Caterpillar C15 come in, and the guy had somehow managed to drop a valve. The piston just hammered it into the cylinder head. It was a complete mess. You have to pull the whole head, check the liner for scoring, make sure the connecting rod isn't bent... it's a massive job. You need the overhead crane just to lift the head off because it weighs like 400 pounds. I usually love getting into those deep engine rebuilds. You have to be so precise with the torque specs and the overhead valve adjustments. If you're off by even a few thousandths of an inch on the injector height, the engine will run like garbage and blow black smoke everywhere. \n\nBut yeah, they moved me to lighter duty for now. And outside of work... I guess I just haven't been doing much. I used to volunteer at church, go to these fitness classes, and I'd always go to the truck-club outings. We used to meet up at the diner on Route 9, a bunch of us with older rigs, just talking shop and drinking terrible coffee. There's this one guy, Stan, who has a beautiful old Peterbilt 359, the classic long-hood with the dual stacks. He actually swapped a mechanical Cummins Big Cam into it, which is just... I mean, the sound of that engine under load is incredible. It doesn't have all the computer nonsense, just pure mechanical fuel injection. You actually have to know how to drive it, how to float the gears without grinding...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm drifting again. What did you ask me? About my days off?\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to make decisions or felt like your mind goes blank, even about things that wouldn't usually bother you?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed my mind going blank or anything like that. I mean, I still make decisions fine, mostly. Like at the shop, even on light duty, you still have to decide the best way to tackle a job. Yesterday I was doing a brake job on this municipal dump truck—an International WorkStar. The rear drums on those things are massive, and the spring brakes can be really dangerous if you don't cage them right before you pull the chamber off. There's a heavy-duty coil spring inside that chamber holding back a couple thousand pounds of force, and if you just unbolt the clamp band without caging it with the T-bolt, that spring will blow the whole housing apart. People have gotten killed by it. It's crazy to think about, really. Just one little bolt is all that stands between you and a trip to the ER. You always have to make sure the threads on the T-bolt aren't stripped, too. I've seen guys try to use rusty ones they found in the bottom of their toolbox, and I always yell at them. It's just not worth the risk for a five-dollar part...\n\nSorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Did you ask about making decisions?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I wouldn't say I'm having trouble thinking clearly or making decisions about normal stuff. I mean, I still know what I want for dinner or what bills need paying, even if the money isn't there for all of them right now. Like, I went to the grocery store the other day and I knew exactly what I needed for this chili recipe I make. It's a really good one, actually. I use a mix of ground chuck and a little bit of spicy Italian sausage, which sounds weird for chili, but it adds this incredible depth of flavor. You have to brown the meat first, obviously, but the trick is to deglaze the pan with a dark stout beer instead of beef broth. It just gives it this rich, earthy taste. And I always toast my own cumin seeds before grinding them. The pre-ground stuff in the little plastic bottles at the store is just sawdust by the time you buy it. You throw the whole seeds in a dry skillet over medium heat for just a couple of minutes until they start smelling fragrant, and then you hit them in a mortar and pestle. It makes a huge difference. I learned that from this guy I used to date years ago, he was a line cook at this fancy steakhouse... 

Wait, what were we talking about? Everyday decisions? Yeah, no, I think I'm fine with that kind of stuff.

Usually, that means there's an issue with the solenoids under the valve cover, or maybe the wiring harness got brittle from the oil heat and cracked. You have to pull the whole top end off just to test the ohms on those solenoids, and getting the gasket to seat right when you put it back together is a real pain if you don't clean the mating surface perfectly. Something about feeling down?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you’re not doing much, you feel unusually tired or like you don’t have much energy?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'm unusually tired... The insulation was worn right through, so every time the engine vibrated, it was shorting out against the metal. Did you ask about my concentration?\n\nDoctor: Over the last few weeks, have you felt down or lost interest in things you usually care about, even when you're not thinking about the foreclosure?\n\nPatient: I don't think I've really felt down, no. Like I said, working on the trucks is keeping me going. It's just the stress of the house, you know? But otherwise, I still care about my work. Actually, I was helping Gary out just this morning with his Freightliner. He's got this Cascadia, one of the newer ones with the DD15 engine. They're decent motors, but the emission systems on them are an absolute nightmare. \n\nHe was getting a derate code for the DEF system—diesel exhaust fluid. The computer thought the tank was empty even though he just filled it. Usually, that means the DEF header unit is shot. It's this long probe that goes down into the tank to measure the level, the temperature, and the quality of the fluid all at once. The problem is, the coolant lines run through it to keep the DEF from freezing in the winter, and if those lines leak inside the tank, it contaminates the whole system. You have to drain the tank, flush the lines, replace the header, and sometimes even swap out the dosing valve if the crystalized DEF clogged it up. Did you ask if I was feeling down?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or lost interest in things outside of work, even when you’re not thinking about the foreclosure or money issues?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. It's a 7.3 Powerstroke—the good year, before they ruined them with all that extra garbage. I was actually out there messing with it this past weekend. \n\nThe high-pressure oil pump is starting to weep a little bit. You can always tell because you start smelling oil and it pools down in the valley pan of the engine block. It's a classic issue with those 7.3s. The O-rings on the HPOP lines just get brittle from all the extreme heat cycles, and eventually, they let go. If you ignore it, you lose pressure and the truck won't even start when it's hot. The piston just hammered it into the cylinder head. It was a complete mess. You have to pull the whole head, check the liner for scoring, make sure the connecting rod isn't bent... it's a massive job. You need the overhead crane just to lift the head off because it weighs like 400 pounds. I usually love getting into those deep engine rebuilds. There's this one guy, Stan, who has a beautiful old Peterbilt 359, the classic long-hood with the dual stacks. He actually swapped a mechanical Cummins Big Cam into it, which is just... I mean, the sound of that engine under load is incredible. It doesn't have all the computer nonsense, just pure mechanical fuel injection. You actually have to know how to drive it, how to float the gears without grinding...\n\nWait, sorry. I'm drifting again. What did you ask me? About my days off?\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to make decisions or felt like your mind goes blank, even about things that wouldn't usually bother you?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed my mind going blank or anything like that. I mean, I still make decisions fine, mostly. Like at the shop, even on light duty, you still have to decide the best way to tackle a job. The rear drums on those things are massive, and the spring brakes can be really dangerous if you don't cage them right before you pull the chamber off. There's a heavy-duty coil spring inside that chamber holding back a couple thousand pounds of force, and if you just unbolt the clamp band without caging it with the T-bolt, that spring will blow the whole housing apart. People have gotten killed by it. It's crazy to think about, really. Just one little bolt is all that stands between you and a trip to the ER. You always have to make sure the threads on the T-bolt aren't stripped, too. I've seen guys try to use rusty ones they found in the bottom of their toolbox, and I always yell at them. It's just not worth the risk for a five-dollar part...\n\nSorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? I mean, the track tension was all out of whack, and the sprockets were just chewed to pieces. The guy who was operating it clearly didn't know how to grease the pivot shafts. You see that a lot with these newer operators—they just climb up in the cab, turn the key, and start pushing dirt, but they never do their daily walkarounds. And with a machine that heavy, if you ignore the track sag, you're going to wear out the front idlers so fast, and those things weigh a ton and cost an absolute fortune to replace. But this one had a weird issue with the Jake brake—the engine retarder. The driver said it was only engaging on three cylinders instead of all six. Usually, that means there's an issue with the solenoids under the valve cover, or maybe the wiring harness got brittle from the oil heat and cracked. Like, pulling a transmission out of an International WorkStar. You're talking about a piece of metal that weighs more than a small car. Even with a transmission jack, you're under there on your back, fighting gravity and trying to line up the splines on the input shaft with the clutch disc. If you're off by even a fraction of an inch, it won't seat, and you're just shoving hundreds of pounds of steel against a brick wall. And the bolts on the bell housing? Half the time they're seized up with road salt and grime, so you're putting your entire body weight into a breaker bar just to bust them loose. The owner thought it was bad fuel, but I knew right away it was something else. You could hear this slight hesitation, almost like a misfire, but not quite. \n\nI hooked up the diagnostic laptop, but honestly, sometimes those codes just send you on a wild goose chase. You have to trust your ears and your hands. I started checking the fuel injectors, and sure enough, the wiring harness going to the number four injector was rubbing against the engine block. The insulation was worn right through, so every time the engine vibrated, it was shorting out against the metal. It's such a simple thing, but if you don't know where to look, you could spend days tearing the fuel system apart. I just wrapped it in some high-temp wire loom and zip-tied it out of the way.
